This yields a closed formula for the common number of edges, and it identifies the bijection of Hibi, Li, Sahara and Shikama as a disjoint union of simple bijections between corresponding classes, explaining why it is a bijection and how it acts on edge directions and lengths. As consequences, the two polytopes have equally many edge directions, with matching multiplicities, and, for every m, equally many pairs of vertices whose symmetric difference has m components; the chain polytope has at least as many edges parallel to each coordinate subspace as the order polytope; and the edge lengths of the chain polytope are dominated by those of the order polytope, with strict inequality in the last two comparisons exactly when the poset contains a three-element chain. We discuss implications for linear and convex combinatorial optimization over ideals and antichains. We also express the number of edges in terms of the comparability graph, extending to stable-set polytopes of all graphs; give a recursion for series-parallel posets and closed formulas for layered, zigzag and crown posets; and characterize the distributive lattices for which the two polytopes are unimodularly equivalent. Finally, either diameter can exceed the other arbitrarily; both are bounded by the width, and they coincide for series-parallel, zigzag and crown posets. △ Less

It expands the application of cardiac vibrational signals and provides new insights into the spatial relationship between cardiac electrical and mechanical activities with spatial location variation. △ Less

    Abstract: …three-channel composite derived from Sentinel-2 Level-2A bands B12, B11 and B8A at 20 m spatial sampling. A fixed linear rendering is applied throughout the dataset. Corresponding masks distinguish background, SWIR-rule active fire and invalid observations. The masks were generated from shortwave-infrared brightness and near-infrared contrast, followed by c… ▽ More This article describes an open image dataset for developing and evaluating active-fire segmentation methods in satellite imagery. The dataset contains 2,148 image-mask pairs from 25 California wildfires, with acquisitions spanning July 2020 to August 2026. Each image is a 512x512-pixel, three-channel composite derived from Sentinel-2 Level-2A bands B12, B11 and B8A at 20 m spatial sampling. A fixed linear rendering is applied throughout the dataset. Corresponding masks distinguish background, SWIR-rule active fire and invalid observations. The masks were generated from shortwave-infrared brightness and near-infrared contrast, followed by constrained neighborhood growth. The release includes chip-level metadata and an incident-disjoint partition containing 18 training, three validation and four test fires. Among the image pairs, 841 contain active-fire labels; these labels occupy 0.0766% of all grid cells. A mask-blind analyst review covers 233 test chips and provides a separate assessment of the rule-generated labels at chip and connected-component levels. Reference training and evaluation code accompanies the data, including a ResNet-34 U-Net implementation with validation-based checkpoint and threshold selection. The archived images, masks, metadata and review annotations support research on rare-class segmentation, learning from algorithmic labels and transfer across fire incidents. The versioned dataset is deposited on Zenodo, with preparation and reuse software maintained in a public GitHub repository. △ Less

    Landscape of scattering universality with general dispersion relations

    Authors: Yidan Wang, Xuesen Na, Michael J. Gullans, Susanne Yelin, Alexey V. Gorshkov

    Abstract: …for contact potentials and analytic monomial dispersions, we analyze the leading low-energy expansion of the scattering phase near these fixed points and extract the corresponding Wigner-delay scaling. Our findings are directly relevant to synthetic quantum systems, where engineered dispersion relations in atomic arrays and photonic crystals offer a platfor… ▽ More Universality in physics describes how disparate systems can exhibit identical low-energy behavior. Here, we reveal a rich landscape of new universal scattering phenomena governed by the interplay between an interaction and a system's density of states. We investigate one-dimensional scattering with dispersion relations $ε(k)=|k|^m$ and $ε(k)=\operatorname{sgn}(k)|k|^m$ for any real $m>1$. For emitter scattering and separable potentials, including contact potentials, we classify the zero-energy $S$-matrix limits. These universal $S$-matrix fixed points depend solely on the dispersion exponent $m$ and a finite set of integers characterizing the low-momentum interaction structure. This classification extends far beyond the standard quadratic-dispersion case ($m=2$). We further derive a generalized Levinson's theorem relating the total winding of the $S$-matrix determinant to the number of bound states. Finally, for contact potentials and analytic monomial dispersions, we analyze the leading low-energy expansion of the scattering phase near these fixed points and extract the corresponding Wigner-delay scaling. Our findings are directly relevant to synthetic quantum systems, where engineered dispersion relations in atomic arrays and photonic crystals offer a platform to explore these universal behaviors. △ Less

    Provably Reliable Classifier Guidance via Cross-Entropy Control

    Authors: Sharan Sahu, Arisina Banerjee, Yuchen Wu

    Abstract: …training. Under mild smoothness assumptions on the classifier, we show that controlling the cross-entropy at each diffusion model step is sufficient to control the corresponding guidance error. In particular, probabilistic classifiers achieving conditional KL divergence $\varepsilon^2$ induce guidance vectors with mean squared error… ▽ More Classifier-guided diffusion models generate conditional samples by augmenting the reverse-time score with the gradient of the log-probability predicted by a probabilistic classifier. In practice, this classifier is usually obtained by minimizing an empirical loss function. While existing statistical theory guarantees good generalization performance when the sample size is sufficiently large, it remains unclear whether such training yields an effective guidance mechanism. We study this question in the context of cross-entropy loss, which is widely used for classifier training. Under mild smoothness assumptions on the classifier, we show that controlling the cross-entropy at each diffusion model step is sufficient to control the corresponding guidance error. In particular, probabilistic classifiers achieving conditional KL divergence $\varepsilon^2$ induce guidance vectors with mean squared error $\widetilde O(d \varepsilon )$, up to constant and logarithmic factors. Our result yields an upper bound on the sampling error of classifier-guided diffusion models and bears resemblance to a reverse log-Sobolev--type inequality. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first result that quantitatively links classifier training to guidance alignment in diffusion models, providing both a theoretical explanation for the empirical success of classifier guidance, and principled guidelines for selecting classifiers that induce effective guidance. △ Less

The resulting evolution is subject to finite-codimensional constraints, under which the state $(y,θ)$ remains on $\mathcal{M}:=\mathcal{M}_1\times\mathcal{M}_2$ throughout the time interval. A Faedo--Galerkin construction provides a unique, global, strong solution to the constrained Boussinesq system for external controls $\mathfrak{H}\in L^2(0,T;\mathbb{H})$, along with uniform energy bounds. In addition, we establish the existence of an optimal triplet $(y^*,θ^*,\mathfrak{H}^*)$ that minimizes the quadratic cost subject to the constrained Boussinesq system. Necessary conditions of the first order are obtained through a coupled state--adjoint--control system. The optimal state $(y^*,θ^*)$ remains on $\mathcal{M}$, while the optimal control lies in the tangent space: $\mathfrak{H}^*(t)\in T_{y^*(t)}\mathcal{M}_1$, for all $t\in[0,T]$. We also show that the map $\mathfrak{H}\mapsto(y_{\mathfrak{H}},θ_{\mathfrak{H}})$ in $(C([0,T];\mathbb{H})\cap L^2(0,T;\mathbb{V}))\times(C([0,T];H)\cap L^2(0,T;V))$ is Fréchet differentiable. The linearized state is the unique weak solution to the constrained linearized Boussinesq system and satisfies the necessary tangency condition. Hence, the well-posedness and first-order optimality conditions are established for the Boussinesq system under nonlinear manifold constraints. △ Less

In contrast, the \(b=1,\ c=-2\) configuration does not exhibit a universal fermiophobic limit. Our results demonstrate that collider constraints on additional neutral gauge bosons depend sensitively on the fermion embeddings and neutral gauge-boson mixing structure, and suggest the possibility of comparatively light \(Z'\) bosons in suitable realizations of extended electroweak gauge theories. △ Less

In this work, we develop new strategies for block-encoding the second-quantized $n$-mode representation of the vibrational Hamiltonian, whose algebraic structure closely parallels that of the second-quantized electronic Hamiltonian. We derive linear-combination-of-unitaries (LCU) representations of this Hamiltonian based on Pauli decomposition and tensor hypercontraction (THC) and construct efficient circuits for the corresponding block encodings. We also extend the Block-Invariant Symmetry Shift (BLISS) technique to vibrational Hamiltonians, yielding a lower LCU 1-norm and, consequently, a lower overall circuit cost. For the molecules considered here, BLISS-THC reduces the Toffoli cost of quantum phase estimation by more than two orders of magnitude relative to existing block-encoding approaches. In comparison with recent Trotter-based methods, it shows up to a ninefold reduction in the T-gate count, with the relative savings tending to increase with system size. Asymptotically, and up to polylogarithmic factors, both the Pauli and THC LCUs trade additional ancillas for a quadratic improvement in scaling with the number of modes, while THC provides an additional quadratic improvement in scaling with the number of modals. These results highlight the importance of Hamiltonian factorizations and Toffoli-qubit tradeoffs for reducing the computational cost of quantum simulations of molecular vibrations. △ Less

    Authors: Anna Krupka, Tom Van Doorsselaere

    Abstract: …correspond to the fast, Alfvén, and slow characteristic families, with the background variables $\boldsymbol{Q}_0^{\pm}$ representing the propagation direction and speed of the corresponding characteristics. In structured plasmas, $α$ may instead be selected from the dispersion relation of a global mode such as a kink… ▽ More The $Q$-variable formalism combines velocity and magnetic field into branches aligned with a selected MHD wave. For homogeneous ideal MHD, appropriate choices of the branch parameter $α$ correspond to the fast, Alfvén, and slow characteristic families, with the background variables $\boldsymbol{Q}_0^{\pm}$ representing the propagation direction and speed of the corresponding characteristics. In structured plasmas, $α$ may instead be selected from the dispersion relation of a global mode such as a kink wave. Recent work reformulated the ideal-MHD Rankine-Hugoniot conditions in $Q$-variables and established their exact equivalence with the classical conservative jump conditions. Here, we apply this formulation to the standard ideal-MHD discontinuity catalogue. We determine how the two discontinuity-frame branches $\boldsymbol{Q}^{\prime+}$ and $\boldsymbol{Q}^{\prime-}$, their sums, and their $α$-normalised differences are constrained across contact, tangential, and rotational discontinuities and across the principal shock families. Material interfaces are distinguished by their normal branch content, rotational discontinuities by an Alfvénic tangential relation, and fast, slow, and intermediate shocks by the magnitude or orientation of the $α$-normalised tangential branch difference. Perpendicular, parallel, switch-on, and switch-off shocks appear as geometrical or limiting cases. The resulting classification expresses the classical discontinuity conditions in a wave-aligned form and, through the characteristic interpretation of $\boldsymbol{Q}_0^{\pm}$, shows how ideal-MHD discontinuities constrain changes in characteristic propagation direction and speed across the interface. It therefore establishes the background interface structure required for subsequent studies of reflection, transmission, coupling, and mode conversion. △ Less

    A Rowhammer attack on Fil-C's software capabilities

    Authors: Amir Naseredini, Suhas Viswanath, Martin Berger

    Abstract: …including prefixes of terminating and non-terminating executions, the protected projection of the probabilistic Rowhammer semantics is the Dirac distribution of the corresponding Rowhammer-free execution. Furthermore, we develop an observation-parametric account of secure information flow. ▽ More Rowhammer is a hardware vulnerability in dynamic random-access memory (DRAM) in which repeated accesses to one or more aggressor rows can induce bit-flips in nearby victim rows. This phenomenon violates a core assumption of conventional programming language semantics: that reading from or writing to one memory location does not modify others. Despite the security importance of this phenomenon, there is no widely established formal framework connecting Rowhammer faults with program behaviour. This makes it difficult to reason rigorously about the efficacy of proposed Rowhammer defences and the program-level guarantees they provide. To address this gap, we present a probabilistic small-step operational semantics for an idealised imperative language subject to Rowhammer-style faults. The semantics is high-level in that it abstracts from DRAM internals and semiconductor physics. A general probabilistic fault model parameterises the semantics, representing Rowhammer-style faults by assigning probabilities to bit-flips during read or write operations within a specified victim region. The resulting distributions are propagated through programs using the standard monadic structure of probabilistic computation. As a case study, we formalise physical separation, a well-known defence that places program variables sufficiently far apart in physical memory that an access to one variable cannot disturb another. We prove a distribution-independent semantic collapse theorem: for every finite execution, including prefixes of terminating and non-terminating executions, the protected projection of the probabilistic Rowhammer semantics is the Dirac distribution of the corresponding Rowhammer-free execution. Furthermore, we develop an observation-parametric account of secure information flow. △ Less

    A Bestiary of Brownian Processes: A Field Guide to Boundary Conditions and Conditioning

    Authors: Lars Lau Raket

    Abstract: The covariance of Brownian motion is the Green kernel of the one-dimensional Laplacian with a pinned left endpoint and a free right endpoint. This correspondence between Green's functions for the Laplacian on an interval and Brownian covariance functions turns out to be a general phenomenon. We use this… ▽ More The covariance of Brownian motion is the Green kernel of the one-dimensional Laplacian with a pinned left endpoint and a free right endpoint. This correspondence between Green's functions for the Laplacian on an interval and Brownian covariance functions turns out to be a general phenomenon. We use this correspondence to organize Brownian-derived Gaussian processes by three operations: choosing a self-adjoint realization, recentering a path, and conditioning a fixed law. Local Robin and selected nonlocal boundary conditions yield a catalogue of twelve processes with closed-form kernels, Brownian-path constructions, spectra and traces. The periodic and Neumann-Neumann realizations annihilate the constants; projecting the source and imposing zero time average selects the corresponding covariance pseudoinverses. Recentering and conditioning can both give a Brownian path zero time average, but the resulting laws differ: a constraint alone does not specify a process. A rank-one conditioning identity gives a scale-invariant criterion for the covariance effect of a shrinking family of observations. For Brownian finite differences of any fixed positive order, the covariance effect vanishes uniformly as the spacing tends to zero. For $m$-fold integrated Brownian motion, derivative constraints of orders $1,\ldots,m$ remain detectable at interior times. For simulation, we identify which grid constructions are exact and which are approximate. △ Less

Most existing systems rely on frontier-scale models. We ask whether a compact model can instead learn this behavior through post-training with real tool interaction. We present ProtAgent, a two-stage pipeline that uses supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to learn the tool interface and standard on-policy REINFORCE to improve the interaction policy. On a fixed closed-loop protocol with real bioinformatics tools, ProtAgent-8B achieves 61.00% success under the annotation-coverage metric, compared with 41.60% for DeepSeek-V3.2-Thinking 671B and 60.80% for the strongest evaluated frontier system. The metric measures recovery of reference annotations under this protocol; it does not by itself establish biological correctness or complete evidence grounding. △ Less

    Late-Time Emergence of Dark Energy and Its Interaction with Dark Matter

    Authors: Sibo Zhang, Weiqiang Yang, Supriya Pan, Olga Mena, Eleonora Di Valentino, Subenoy Chakraborty

    Abstract: …the interaction parameter is shifted away from zero at more than 95\% CL whenever the CMB data are combined with any of these additional probes, with the preferred direction corresponding to a transfer of energy from DE to DM. While CMB alone yields a high value of $H_0$, in agreement with local determinations, this effect is reduced when DESI is added and d… ▽ More We present an interacting scenario between dark energy (DE) and dark matter (DM), where DE has an emergent nature, that means, DE was absent in the early universe but it becomes effective only at late times. We consider two specific emergent DE models, one with no free parameters and the other featuring two parameters describing the speed and epoch of the transition. We constrain both scenarios using the cosmic microwave background (CMB) measurements from the Planck 2018 release, baryon acoustic oscillations from DESI DR2, and three different compilations of Type Ia supernovae (PantheonPlus, DES-Dovekie, and Union3). Our analysis indicates that current cosmological probes are not able to tightly constrain the speed of the transition. For both scenarios, the posterior distribution of the interaction parameter is shifted away from zero at more than 95\% CL whenever the CMB data are combined with any of these additional probes, with the preferred direction corresponding to a transfer of energy from DE to DM. While CMB alone yields a high value of $H_0$, in agreement with local determinations, this effect is reduced when DESI is added and disappears once supernova data are included. In contrast, the clustering parameter $S_8$ is consistently shifted toward lower values in the combined datasets, and it is correlated with the preference for a negative interaction. However, according to the $Δχ^2_{\rm min}$ and Bayesian evidence, none of the interacting models is favored over $Λ$CDM or $w_0w_a$CDM, indicating that the interaction does not rescue these emergent DE models. Our results therefore highlight the limitations of these scenarios in addressing current cosmological tensions, while pointing to the crucial role of future data in further assessing their viability. △ Less

    cs.LG cs.AI

    A Distribution-Matching Perspective on LLM Training Algorithms

    Authors: Yanxing Huang, Bingzhang Yan, Zihan Tang, Wenzhe Li, Peng Li, Yang Liu

    Abstract: …between the current model and a target distribution, and that characterizing the properties of this target distribution provides substantial insight into the behavior of the corresponding training algorithms. By analyzing specific forms of the target distribution, we establish that On-Policy Distillation (OPD) enjoys an advantage over classical Knowledge Dis… ▽ More In this work, we propose a unified framework for training algorithms across different stages of modern large language model development, based on the perspective of distribution matching. We show that a broad class of language model training algorithms can be interpreted as minimizing a certain divergence between the current model and a target distribution, and that characterizing the properties of this target distribution provides substantial insight into the behavior of the corresponding training algorithms. By analyzing specific forms of the target distribution, we establish that On-Policy Distillation (OPD) enjoys an advantage over classical Knowledge Distillation (KD) when the current model distribution is close to the target distribution, while exhibiting a disadvantage when the two distributions are far apart. Furthermore, when the target distribution itself depends on the model being optimized, we show that the accumulation of estimation variance can induce a deterministic degradation of the target distribution. These results provide a theoretical explanation, at least in part, for the long-term behavior of existing Reinforcement Learning (RL) algorithms. With this intuition we further designed Conditional KL (CKL) regularization for mitigating the degradation of the target distribution. CKL can effectively preserve mode diversity while relaxing the constraint imposed by classical KL regularization on reward improvements. We believe this theoretical perspective can bring some new understanding of language model training algorithms and provide useful guidance for future research. △ Less

The Hochschild theory of multiplicative sequences is introduced as a functor taking values in graded algebras in the symmetric monoidal category of chain complexes, constructed with the help of the shuffle product.     Quantum Closed Loop Inverse Kinematics Through Hilbert-Space Encoding of Robot Kinematics

    Authors: Andrea Morghen, Pierluigi Arpenti, Roberto Schiattarella, Giovanni Acampora, Bruno Siciliano

    Abstract: …of robot motion. This paper introduces a structure-preserving quantum representation of rigid-body transformations and serial-manipulator kinematics, establishing a correspondence between classical kinematic models and parameterized quantum circuits. Building on the Bloch-sphere representation, spatial directions are encoded into single-qubit states, while q… ▽ More The integration of quantum computing into robotics requires mathematical representations that preserve the geometric structure and configuration dependence of robot motion. This paper introduces a structure-preserving quantum representation of rigid-body transformations and serial-manipulator kinematics, establishing a correspondence between classical kinematic models and parameterized quantum circuits. Building on the Bloch-sphere representation, spatial directions are encoded into single-qubit states, while quantum rotations reproduce the ordered composition of reference-frame transformations. Translational contributions are combined through quantum-state aggregation, with their metric scales retained classically, enabling the reconstruction of end-effector position and orientation from Pauli expectation values. The formulation preserves the original joint variables and accommodates different kinematic conventions and joint types. Beyond forward kinematics, we establish that the manipulator geometric Jacobian can be reconstructed from quantum-observable derivatives using the parameter-shift rule, with analytical equivalence to its classical counterpart under ideal readout. To demonstrate the applicability of this representation to robot-level algorithms, we introduce Quantum Closed Loop Inverse Kinematics, a hybrid closed-loop method that exploits quantum-derived pose and Jacobian information. The proposed framework provides a geometrically interpretable foundation for developing and investigating quantum and hybrid computational methods for robotic kinematics and motion. △ Less

    HorizonStream: Long-Horizon Attention for Streaming 3D Reconstruction

    Authors: Chong Cheng, Peilin Tao, Nanjie Yao, Guanzhi Ding, Xianda Chen, Yuansen Du, Xiaoyang Guo, Wei Yin, Weiqiang Ren, Qian Zhang, Zhengqing Chen, Hao Wang

    Abstract: …on long sequences. We trace these failures to a fundamental mismatch. However, current architectures impose uniform and pathological influence patterns. For example, sliding windows enforce hard cutoffs, while ungated… ▽ More Online 3D reconstruction requires estimating camera pose and scene geometry under strict causal and bounded-memory constraints. Existing methods often suffer from drift, jitter, or collapse on long sequences. We trace these failures to a fundamental mismatch. Streaming geometry is inherently temporally heterogeneous, with evidence ranging from short-lived correspondences to persistent global scale. However, current architectures impose uniform and pathological influence patterns. For example, sliding windows enforce hard cutoffs, while ungated recurrence and causal attention cause cache saturation and spike-like attention sinks. To resolve this, we formalize geometric propagation as an \emph{evidence influence kernel} and propose HorizonStream, a long-horizon Transformer that explicitly factorizes this kernel. For the long-range temporal factor, Geometric Linear Attention learns channel-wise decay rates to enable bounded, multi-timescale propagation of geometric evidence. For the short-range spatial factor, Geometric Local Attention with Spatiotemporal RoPE performs reliable 3D matching while suppressing attention sinks. Finally, Metric Readout Tokens recover stable scale and rigid pose directly from the persistent geometric state. Extensive experiments show that HorizonStream, trained on only 48-frame clips, generalizes stably to sequences exceeding 10,000\ frames with constant memory and linear time, achieving state-of-the-art streaming 3D reconstruction performance. These models are deployed in the semi-classical limit, where the collective local response over many lattice sites is adequate. However, they fail to capture the full spatial dispersion, which requires a lattice-resolved description of light. We demonstrate our theory for a two-dimensional (2D) tight-binding model of a gapped honeycomb lattice. For IGEV on Scene Flow, geometric edge pixels account for only 6% of evaluated pixels but about half of the total absolute disparity error. Photometric edges can respond to texture changes without corresponding depth discontinuities, making them unreliable cues for boundary refinement. We propose E3Stereo, which learns geometric edges from disparity supervision and combines these boundary cues with the backbone disparity and image details to predict full-resolution disparity corrections. The same edge map also emphasizes boundary errors during training, while joint optimization allows the edge predictor to adapt to disparity refinement. On the Scene Flow dataset, E3Stereo reduces end-point error (EPE) at depth boundaries by 9.5%-14.3% across RAFT-Stereo, IGEV, and MonSter, while also reducing overall EPE. After fine-tuning, our IGEV-based model improves upon IGEV on all eleven reported metrics across ETH3D, KITTI 2012, and KITTI 2015. The source code will be made publicly available upon acceptance. △ Less

    Noninteger completely monotonic degrees of Stirling remainders and their derivatives

    Authors: Valmir Krasniqi

    Abstract: …real-degree conjectures recorded by Mahmoud and Qi for the signed remainders of the logarithm of the gamma function and their derivatives. We give a unified proof of the corresponding integer-degree formula, recovering known cases and resolving Zhu's conjectures for higher derivatives in this range. Every real completely monotonic degree lies strictly be… ▽ More For Stirling expansions truncated after at least two Bernoulli terms, we disprove the three real-degree conjectures recorded by Mahmoud and Qi for the signed remainders of the logarithm of the gamma function and their derivatives. We give a unified proof of the corresponding integer-degree formula, recovering known cases and resolving Zhu's conjectures for higher derivatives in this range. Every real completely monotonic degree lies strictly between the predicted integer and the next integer. We obtain a universal fractional lower bound through an explicit inverse function and a simpler rational bound valid for every derivative order. A local estimate for the negative part of the Laplace kernel's derivative with respect to its logarithmic variable makes the lower improvement proportional to the reciprocal of the combined truncation and derivative order. Together with a uniform upper bound, this determines the exact order of decay of the fractional excess for each fixed derivative order and for its supremum over all derivative orders. Sharper kernel estimates give stronger bounds for the first two degrees. The proof combines fractional positivity criteria, the classical Laplace representation, a sign characterization of the associated densities, and a convexity obstruction at the upper integer endpoint. All estimates used in the proofs are analytic. The exact real degrees and the optimal asymptotic constants remain open. △ Less

When used for knowledge tracing, they improve predictive performance by… ▽ More Educational taxonomies organize educational content, such as exercise problems, into concepts, which are also called skills or knowledge components. These taxonomies define learning objectives in educational applications such as intelligent tutoring systems. Developed through human expertise and iterative refinement, they also provide fundamental structure for curricula, assessment, and models of student learning. Recent advances in semantic text embeddings make it possible to construct alternative educational taxonomies computationally from the content itself. In this paper, we investigate this possibility by clustering embeddings of math questions to construct embedding-based educational taxonomies. We compare these taxonomies with the existing Common Core State Standards (CCSS)-based knowledge-component taxonomies in two ASSISTments datasets and evaluate their utility in Deep Knowledge Tracing, a downstream task that relies on taxonomic mappings of questions to knowledge components. The embedding-based taxonomies exhibit substantial but incomplete correspondence with the existing taxonomies, revealing both preserved and alternative organizations of educational content. When used for knowledge tracing, they improve predictive performance by approximately 3-5 AUC percentage points. These results demonstrate the potential of semantic embeddings for automatically constructing educational taxonomies that both recover structure present in human-developed taxonomies and provide useful alternative representations for downstream educational modeling. △ Less

Neural operators can accelerate these computations by learning solution operators from offline simulation data. However, conventional DeepONet surrogates rely on fixed-grid inputs and affine reconstruction, limiting their flexibility across resolutions and their approximation accuracy at a fixed latent dimension. In this paper, we develop a transformer-based DeepONet for multi-resolution RTE surrogate modelling. The proposed approach combines a Perceiver encoder, which maps temperature and absorption coefficient fields at different spatial resolutions into a fixed-size latent representation, with a cross-attention decoder that enables non-affine reconstruction of the radiative intensity. This architecture allows a single model to process inputs at varying resolutions without resampling them onto a common grid. We formulate a computable empirical version of a previously derived theoretical lower bound for affine reconstruction, enabling direct comparison with model errors on the same dataset. Experiments on a two-dimensional pool-fire dataset demonstrate that the proposed approach outperforms autoencoder-based DeepONets in predicting both radiative intensity and incident radiation, with fewer trainable parameters. Power spectral density analysis shows improved preservation of small-scale structures. Both affine baselines remain above the corresponding lower bound at all evaluated ranks, whereas the proposed model achieves a training error below it at one matched latent dimension, demonstrating the potential of non-affine reconstruction to overcome this representational limitation. Experiments on a dataset combining three spatial resolutions confirm the accuracy advantage of the approach at each input resolution. △ Less

    Sharp minimax risks and phase transitions in sparse submatrix detection

    Authors: Subhajit Goswami, Rajarshi Mukherjee

    Abstract: …. Each of these rates corresponds to the risk of a suitably calibrated scan or sum test, whence follow the upper bounds. The main problem is to derive the matching regime-specific lower bounds. The mixture likelihood ratio is an average rather than a product and thus its logarithm lacks the additive structure required by standard large deviation formulatio… ▽ More In this paper, we study the minimax risk of detecting a sparse, elevated-mean Gaussian submatrix planted in a larger noisy matrix. When the planted submatrix has size $n\times n$ and the ambient matrix has size $N\times N$ with $N=n^{1+α}$, the seminal work of Butucea and Ingster (2013) identifies the sharp detection boundary around which the risk converges to $0$ or $1$. We extend that zero-one theory by determining sharp risk asymptotics throughout a two-parameter phase diagram. Above the boundary, the risk decays stretched- or super-exponentially fast, and we determine its precise exponent. Below the boundary, where the risk tends to $1$, we obtain matching absolute-constant bounds for its polynomial rate of convergence. In both regimes, the asymptotics change across the line $α+δ=1/2$, where $δ$ parametrizes the signed distance from the detection boundary. Finally, on the boundary, we show that the minimax risk converges to the nondegenerate constant $\frac12$ in a very sparse regime where $n$ remains fixed and $N\to\infty$. Each of these rates corresponds to the risk of a suitably calibrated scan or sum test, whence follow the upper bounds. The main problem is to derive the matching regime-specific lower bounds. The mixture likelihood ratio is an average rather than a product and thus its logarithm lacks the additive structure required by standard large deviation formulations. On the other hand, classical second-moment estimates fail to be sharp due to overlap-induced correlations. To address these issues, we develop two general lower-bound principles based on truncated mixture likelihoods, tailored to the target risks on both sides of the detection boundary. The same framework yields sharp risk asymptotics for sparse subtensors with equal side lengths and also applies to more general testing problems including the detection of rectangular subtensors. △ Less

    Local-to-Global Exactness of SDP Relaxations for Sparse QCQPs

    Authors: Masakazu Kojima, Sunyoung Kim, Naohiko Arima

    Abstract: …quadratically constrained quadratic program (QCQP). The SDP relaxation is exact if, whenever it has an optimal solution, it admits a rank-at-most-one optimal solution that corresponds to an optimal solution of the QCQP. Using the maximal cliques of a chordal extension of the aggregate sparsity pattern graph of the data matrices, we formulate the SDP relaxati… ▽ More We study exact semidefinite programming (SDP) relaxation for a given sparse quadratically constrained quadratic program (QCQP). The SDP relaxation is exact if, whenever it has an optimal solution, it admits a rank-at-most-one optimal solution that corresponds to an optimal solution of the QCQP. Using the maximal cliques of a chordal extension of the aggregate sparsity pattern graph of the data matrices, we formulate the SDP relaxation in terms of clique-wise matrix variables and develop a local-to-global framework for certifying exactness. For each clique-wise matrix variable, we introduce a local sub-SDP with two parameters: a local right-hand-side vector and a consistency matrix specifying the values of entries shared by overlapping clique-wise matrix variables. In the main theorem, these parameters are determined by an optimal solution of the global clique-wise SDP. The theorem shows that if the resulting local sub-SDPs are exact, then the original SDP relaxation is exact. Under the additional assumption that any two distinct cliques intersect in at most one node, we present three classes of local QCQPs that can be incorporated into this framework: convex local QCQPs, local QCQPs characterized by sign-pattern conditions, and separable local QCQPs with a limited number of constraints. Examples illustrate how these different local QCQP classes can be combined in sparse QCQPs. △ Less

Based on this framework, we prove approximate controllability of the original system by relating it to the controllability of the associated linearized inclusion and constructing appropriate controls involving the duality mapping. Finally, we present applications to illustrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach. △ Less

    Beta-shifts without typical periodic optimization

    Authors: Wen Huang, Oliver Jenkinson, Leiye Xu, Yiwei Zhang

    Abstract: …known examples of beta-shifts without TPO, and, since they have the specification property, the first known examples of shift spaces with specification but without TPO. The corresponding beta-transformations do not have TPO in any space of Hölder functions on the interval. The main ingredient in the proofs is a rigidity property of Sturmian subshifts: modulo… ▽ More We show that beta-shifts whose lexicographically largest point is a Sturmian sequence do not have typical periodic optimization (TPO): on each such beta-shift there is a non-empty open set of Lipschitz functions whose unique maximizing measure is the Sturmian measure, which is not supported on a periodic orbit. These are the first known examples of beta-shifts without TPO, and, since they have the specification property, the first known examples of shift spaces with specification but without TPO. The corresponding beta-transformations do not have TPO in any space of Hölder functions on the interval. The main ingredient in the proofs is a rigidity property of Sturmian subshifts: modulo constants and Lipschitz coboundaries, the space of Lipschitz functions on such a subshift is one-dimensional. We also show that the set of $β>1$ for which the beta-shift or the beta-transformation does not have TPO is dense in $(1,\infty)$, but has zero packing dimension. The density is proved by a tuning construction, analogous to Douady--Hubbard tuning in the Mandelbrot set, which produces non-Sturmian subshifts with the same rigidity property. Finally, we show that every beta-shift has typical zero-entropy optimization: for an open dense set of Lipschitz functions, the maximizing measure is unique and its support has zero topological entropy. △ Less

    Geometry-Aware Epipolar Correspondence Graphs for Monocular Visual Odometry

    Authors: Prateeth Rao, Sachit Rao

    Abstract: …Graph Representation Learning to address challenges that arise in visual odometry. Employing an existing detector-free model to find match… ▽ More We demonstrate Graph Representation Learning to address challenges that arise in visual odometry. In this context, the change in pose of a camera is typically estimated using matched correspondences in frames that satisfy the epipolar constraint; finding this unique set under different situations is not a simple task. Employing an existing detector-free model to find matching correspondences in a pair of images, a correspondence graph is constructed whose topology is guided by the epipolar constraint. Starting with a graph where every node is connected to $k$ closest neighbours (by distance), this graph is pruned based on an initial estimate of the Essential Matrix; this feature is in stark contrast with traditional graph formulations where graphs from local spatial neighborhoods defined by heuristics are constructed. The graph learning module is then trained to generate an aggregated representative vector that summarises the geometric context of the graph. This vector forms an input to a Multi-layer Perceptron (MLP), whose outputs are standard rotation and translation elements that define the change in pose. To investigate the effect of graph learning on this representation, four variations of Graph Neural Networks (GNNs): GCN, GAT, GIN, and Graph-based Cross Attention are evaluated under a unified learning framework. The application to monocular visual odometry is validated using outdoor benchmark datasets such as KiTTi, ETH3D, King's College, and Tartan Air. Experimental results demonstrate consistent improvements in trajectory and relative pose estimation across diverse metrics when compared with classical consensus-based estimators and learning-guided correspondence methods. △ Less

The Caputo derivative is discretized by the L1 formula, while the nonlinear nonlocal flux is approximated by a conservative upwind finite-difference scheme. We establish several structural properties of the implicit scheme in arbitrary space dimension, including a discrete maximum principle, non-negativity, mass conservation, $\ell^\infty$ and $\ell^2$ stability, existence of non-negative discrete solutions, and a discrete energy-dissipation estimate. For rigorous convergence analysis, we restrict ourselves to one space dimension and introduce the cumulative variable associated with the density. This transforms the discrete problem into a monotone approximation of an equation involving the fractional Laplacian. Interestingly, for semigroup-based pressure-gradient weights, the resulting discrete operator is identified with the fractional discrete Laplacian. Next, using comparison and contraction properties of the integrated scheme, for $m\geq2$ and under the stated spatial regularity and weighted temporal consistency assumptions, we prove uniform convergence of the cumulative numerical solution and convergence of the corresponding density against compactly supported smooth test functions. The temporal estimates allow a weak singularity at the initial time. We also analyze an explicit variant of the scheme and derive a sufficient CFL condition covering the full range $0<σ<1$. △ Less

Unlike static crack detection, which can now be largely automated using computer vision, vision-based crack tracking is a coupled cross-view and cross-time problem: the same physical crack must retain its identity as camera viewpoints change, while newly developed growth must be distinguished from existing crack segments in a unified reference view. Existing segmentation and 3D reconstruction methods can recover crack masks or scene geometry, but they do not preserve crack identity across views or quantify temporal extension. To address this gap, this study formulates crack growth monitoring as a cross-view and cross-time crack-instance tracking task and proposes a geometry-assisted, morphology-aware framework. The framework first uses image-based geometric registration to align observations from different viewpoints into a shared structural reference, then applies morphology-aware instantiation to assign physically interpretable crack identities, and finally performs temporal mapping in the unified view to distinguish newly developed growth from existing crack segments. To enable evaluation, a temporally annotated dataset was established and used, covering three representative cracking scenarios: single dominant crack propagation, mixed-mode branching, and multiple-crack interaction, with pixel-level masks and cross-time instance labels. Experimental results show that the proposed framework can establish loose cross-view identity correspondence under changing viewpoints, while strict spatial overlap and growth localization remain limited by early micro-crack segmentability and projection drift, highlighting directions for future crack-monitoring research. △ Less

    Large spin large deviations for interacting particle systems

    Authors: Cristian Giardinà, Tomohiro Sasamoto

    Abstract: …The rate function is described in terms of a Hamiltonian on a lattice in terms of classical spin variables. Notably, for our main model, the symmetric harmonic process, the corresponding Hamiltonian is identified with the classical integrable non-compact Ishimori spin chain. Furthermore, we show that our large spin large deviation theory is fully consiste… ▽ More We propose a novel framework for analyzing the sample-path large deviations of interacting particle systems directly on a lattice by considering a large-spin limit ($s \to \infty$). Through an appropriate scaling of variables, the process concentrates around its deterministic trajectory, governed by the lattice diffusion equation. We quantify the exponential cost of observing atypical trajectories by explicitly computing the path-space large deviation rate function from the scaled microscopic generator, based on a theory due to Feng and Kurtz. The rate function is described in terms of a Hamiltonian on a lattice in terms of classical spin variables. Notably, for our main model, the symmetric harmonic process, the corresponding Hamiltonian is identified with the classical integrable non-compact Ishimori spin chain. Furthermore, we show that our large spin large deviation theory is fully consistent with traditional Macroscopic Fluctuation Theory (MFT), smoothly recovering the standard MFT Hamiltonian in the spatial continuous limit. We also perform a Poisson-noise Martin--Siggia--Rose (MSR) computation to derive the non-linear lattice Hamiltonian directly from microscopic dynamics, use a relative entropy argument to obtain the density-current action, and establish the lattice fluctuation theorem alongside its symmetry origin. Overall, our framework provides a naturally regularized, lattice analogue of MFT that circumvents spatial ultraviolet singularities and possible continuous density blow-ups. We also apply our new scheme to partial symmetric exclusion process, symmetric inclusion processes, Brownian energy process and integrable heat conduction model, showing how their large-deviation Hamiltonians relate to classical compact and non-compact spin chains. △ Less

We ask whether five frozen, independently trained models with different internal designs (four transformers from four organizations and one state-space model) can be related through their activations alone, with no weight merging. We use this to build a shared space with Generalized Procrustes Analysis, in which no model is a fixed reference. At the final layer, mean held-out retrieval accuracy over the twenty ordered model pairs is 0.91 to 0.93 on two probe sets (chance 0.03), beating pairwise linear maps (0.82 to 0.85), but the space is worse than those maps in the middle layers. It transfers to ordinary English of unfamiliar grammatical form and fails on mathematical and logical notation. We then test whether the space supports a router that picks among the five models on gradeable tasks. Across four task types we find no case where routing beats always using the strongest model, and we report this as a negative result. A correctness signal that looked strong turned out to be category membership in disguise. All experiments ran on one laptop without a GPU. △ Less

    Authors: Jianfeng Hou, Caiyun Hu, Xizhi Liu, Yixiao Zhang

    Abstract: …. A natural and fundamental problem is to determine the corresponding average-degree (or density) thresholds. This problem turns out to be substantially more difficult. The case $r=3$ was settled for large $n$ by Allen, Böttcher, Hladký, and Piguet~\cite{ABHP15}, who remarkably identified f… ▽ More The exact minimum-degree thresholds that guarantee the existence of $k$ vertex-disjoint copies of the complete graph $K_r$ in an $n$-vertex graph were determined in the celebrated theorems of Dirac (1952) for $r=2$, Corrádi--Hajnal (1963) for $r=3$, and Hajnal--Szemerédi (1970) for general $r \ge 4$. A natural and fundamental problem is to determine the corresponding average-degree (or density) thresholds. This problem turns out to be substantially more difficult. The seminal theorem of Erdős--Gallai (1959) resolved the case $r=2$. The case $r=3$ was settled for large $n$ by Allen, Böttcher, Hladký, and Piguet~\cite{ABHP15}, who remarkably identified four distinct classes of extremal constructions arising in different ranges of $k$. They further posed the problem of establishing a density analogue of the Hajnal--Szemerédi theorem, noting that ``We are not even sure what the complete family of extremal graphs should be.'' In this paper, we establish an asymptotic density Hajnal-SzemerHedi theorem for \(K_4\) by determining \(\mathrm{ex}(n,(k+1)K_4)\) up to an additive \(O(n)\) term for all integers \(n\ge 4k\ge 0\). Five families of constructions show that this bound is sharp up to an additive \(O(n)\) term. For general \(r\ge 5\), we propose a candidate family consisting of \(r+1\) extremal construction classes. △ Less

    Authors: Heling Deng, Ken D. Olum, Rutger van Haasteren

    Abstract: …, with a diagonal Fourier-domain covariance determined by the power spectral density at the corresponding frequencies. Although this rank-reduced modeling significantly accelerates likelihood evaluation and provides a good approximation in many scenarios, it can lead to biased parameter inference because it is insensitive to spectral features on scales smaller than $\sim 0.1~\mathrm{yr}^{-1}$. We introduce a better construction of the low-rank covariance for low-frequency processes by maximizing the log-likelihood averaged over an ensemble of realizations drawn from the true model likelihood. In this approach, the basis-domain covariance matrix is sensitive to the entire model spectrum and is generally non-diagonal. Using two mock datasets, we demonstrate improved likelihood accuracy and more faithful recovery of spectral features compared to the conventional Fourier-diagonal model. Benchmark tests show that this new model requires a computational cost comparable to that of the conventional approach. △ Less

    MS-COOT: Comparing Morse-Smale Complexes with Co-Optimal Transport

    Authors: Guangyu Meng, Mingzhe Li, Erin Wolf Chambers

    Abstract: …into regions induced by gradient flow. However, existing approaches typically rely on graph-based representations, matching critical points without explicitly establishing correspondences between regions. In this work, we represent the MS complex as a hypergraph, where critical points form nodes and regions define hyperedges. We introduce MS-COOT, a co-optim… ▽ More Understanding and comparing structures in scalar fields is a central challenge in scientific visualization, with applications ranging from feature analysis to temporal and structural comparison. The Morse-Smale (MS) complex provides a natural representation by decomposing a scalar field into regions induced by gradient flow. However, existing approaches typically rely on graph-based representations, matching critical points without explicitly establishing correspondences between regions. In this work, we represent the MS complex as a hypergraph, where critical points form nodes and regions define hyperedges. We introduce MS-COOT, a co-optimal transport distance that jointly computes correspondences between critical points and regions. This formulation enables explicit region-to-region matching within a distance-based framework, allowing identification of region-level events such as splitting and merging. We instantiate this framework with domain-specific components, including a hypernetwork function encoding relationships between critical points and regions, persistence-based probability measures, and a sample cost term that incorporates critical point attributes. We evaluate MS-COOT on datasets spanning 2D simulations, 3D surface meshes, and volumetric data. Our results show that MS-COOT provides distinct characterization of certain region-level structural changes compared to graph-based distances, achieves competitive accuracy in shape classification, and improves classification of simulation parameter settings in a 3D ensemble. △ Less

This paper constructs convex inner approximations that connect nonlinear network feasibility, constrained device response, and dispatch-interval tracking. A fixed-point certificate enforces AC operating limits under bounded load uncertainty. Componentwise response envelopes convert heterogeneous device dynamics into affine conditions on measured states and power references. A command governor then combines these conditions with available ramping capability and a hard terminal-error bound. It selects an admissible target through a minimum-deviation objective and holds the corresponding reference while reserved fast regulation remains active. The resulting quadratic program optimizes controllable references and auxiliary variables. Tests on the IEEE 39- and 118-bus systems include 96 single-target executions and 64 transitions in continuous dispatch sequences at five- and fifteen-minute intervals. All 160 transitions satisfy the AC constraints, independent balancing-generator capability checks, and the prescribed regulation-adjusted terminal accuracy. Complete admission-cycle medians are 3.45 and 8.79 ms. A connected synthetic system with 3,200 buses and 1,000 generating units extends the computational assessment to 999 independent references, with a median cycle time of \LargeMedian{} s. The construction provides a tractable link between available ramping capability and safe, timely execution of AC dispatch. △ Less

The coefficient of shear viscosity, $η$ is observed to have a large enhancement in the presence of finite strangeness in the… ▽ More We study the diffusion and shear viscosity coefficients of hot isospin asymmetric strange hadronic matter. The effects due to the baryon density, isospin, strangeness, and temperature on the nucleons and hyperons are studied within a chiral SU(3) model. The medium modifications of the baryons arise due to interactions with the mean scalar and vector fields within the model. The thermodynamic and transport properties are studied in the hot strange hadronic matter. The diffusion matrix associated with the multiple charges (baryon number, isospin, and strangeness), as well as the coefficient of shear viscosity, are computed from the Boltzmann equation using first-order Chapman--Enskog expansion within the relaxation time approximation. The diffusion coefficient, $κ_{QQ}$ corresponding to the conserved electric charge has also been studied in the present work. There are observed to be significant effects from the isospin asymmetry as well as the strangeness of the medium on the diffusion coefficients. The coefficient of shear viscosity, $η$ is observed to have a large enhancement in the presence of finite strangeness in the medium due to additional contributions from the hyperons. The effects due to isospin asymmetry on the shear viscosity coefficient is however observed to be marginal both in nuclear and hyperonic matter. The present study can be relevant for the experimental observables of asymmetric relativistic heavy-ion collisions, e.g., in the compressed baryonic matter (CBM) experiment at the FAIR facility at GSI as well as in the future J-PARC-HI program. △ Less

By removing a thin neig… ▽ More We study a two-dimensional Schrödinger equation with a boundary degeneracy. More precisely, the diffusion matrix is given by $ A=\operatorname{diag}(1,x_2^α), α\in(0,1)$, so that the partial differential operator degenerates on a portion of the boundary. The main objective is to establish a boundary observability inequality for the corresponding degenerate Schrödinger equation. To overcome the difficulties caused by the boundary degeneracy, we introduce a shape design and approximation procedure. By removing a thin neighborhood of the degenerate boundary, we construct a family of uniformly Schrödinger equations on perturbed domains. We first investigate the spectral properties of the associated operators and establish the convergence of the approximate problems to the original degenerate problem. We then derive a boundary observability inequality for the uniformly approximating equations and pass to the limit as the thickness of the removed boundary layer tends to zero. This yields a boundary observability inequality for the original degenerate Schrödinger equation. Our approach combines spectral analysis, separation of variables, and shape design techniques. By employing multiplier methods for the uniformly approximating problems and passing to the degenerate limit, we establish the boundary observability inequality for the original degenerate Schrödinger equation. △ Less

The number of charged particles passing through each EPD ring is extracted using convoluted Landau fits to the deposited energy spectra. To account for the detector's response to the primary particles, these measured EPD hit numbers are unfolded using response matrices determined via Monte Carlo simulations. The response matrix is calculated separately for each energy and centrality class using the UrQMD event generator embedded into the GEANT3 simulation for the STAR detector. The new STAR measurements are consistent with the previous PHOBOS results in the corresponding rapidity intervals at 19.6 and 200 GeV, while providing improved precision. In addition, the new data extend the energy coverage to 7.7--200 GeV across multiple centrality classes. This expanded dataset enables a more comprehensive test of the limiting fragmentation hypothesis from central to peripheral collisions. The measurements are also compared with theoretical model calculations, including UrQMD, HIJING, and AMPT, in order to investigate the underlying physics implications. △ Less

Yet, accurate event-based state estimation remains difficult: existing systems often use events as a complement to frames or rely on feature tracking, correspondence search, and optimization-heavy backends, limiting the efficiency promised by event sensing. We introduce Tight Learned Event-Inertial Odometry (TLEIO), a compact filter-based pipeline that learns short-window relative motion directly from voxelized event streams and tightly fuses the resulting constraints with IMU propagation. Its learned front-end, EventsFormer, predicts local displacements and per-axis uncertainty from event voxels in a single forward pass, while a stochastic-cloning EKF fuses these overlapping predictions with IMU propagation, producing metric trajectories without explicit feature tracks and pose optimization at inference time. The network is trained directly from pose displacements, without requiring event-flow labels. We evaluate TLEIO on the TartanAir/TartanEvent benchmark and on real DAVIS240c sequences. On TartanAir, TLEIO reduces average ATE from 2.26 m to 1.10 m over the IMU-only TLIO baseline, while achieving up to 48.9% lower runtime than optimization-based event odometry methods. △ Less

    Real Time Motion Detection in Video Inspired by Photonic Time Stretch

    Authors: Callen MacPhee, Yiming Zhou, Bahram Jalali

    Abstract: …the time series spectrum and, after group delay dispersion, induces a temporal phase that exists only if there is motion. Spatiotemporal group delay dispersion templates, corresponding to dielectric functions, classify specific motion trajectories. Unlike conventional empirical phase-based motion-analysis pipelines that require narrow-band spatial channeliza… ▽ More Motion perception is fundamental to computer vision and physical AI, and plays a central role in robotics, autonomous vehicles, human-computer interaction, and video compression. Traditional approaches such as background subtraction, frame-differencing and optical flow algorithms often struggle in complex or dynamic scenes, typically sacrificing spatial resolution for computational tractability. Here we introduce MoViD (Motion estimation via Virtual dispersion and phase Detection), a physics-inspired algorithm grounded in the principles that govern photonic time stretch - a highly successful ultrafast realtime data acquisition platform. Drawing inspiration from the optical physics therein, MoViD treats video as a collection of optical time series waveforms associated with pixels. Motion of an object through a pixel broadens the time series spectrum and, after group delay dispersion, induces a temporal phase that exists only if there is motion. Spatiotemporal group delay dispersion templates, corresponding to dielectric functions, classify specific motion trajectories. Unlike conventional empirical phase-based motion-analysis pipelines that require narrow-band spatial channelization and pyramid reconstruction, the physics-based MoViD operates directly on the broadband video signal, vastly increasing its computational speed. The resulting algorithm achieves unprecedented performance in both motion characterization and motion magnification, providing full-resolution motion mapping in real time at nearly 200 frames per second at 4K resolution and 70 frames per second for 8K video - orders of magnitude faster than classical optical flow approaches or learning-based methods. Notably, such extreme performance enables real time motion analysis at ultrahigh resolution for motion detection and compensation in robotics and other autonomous devices. △ Less

    Junction Hook Errors in the Color Code

    Authors: Salahedeen Issa, Margaret Martonosi

    Abstract: …anyon strings of two different colors at the point where the three colored strings of a logical operator meet, which we call the junction. As such, the hook error does not correspond to a single type of anyon, yet it still reduces the distance. For distances $d=7,9,11$, we verify that every non-corner plaquette admits a scheduled hook error that, together wi… ▽ More Color-dependent syndrome extraction schedules have been proposed to mitigate hook errors in the triangular color code. Recent work by Kishony and Fowler (arXiv:2603.28852) identifies distance-reducing hook errors by the type of anyon for which they provide a shortcut and chooses the CNOT ordering for stabilizers of each color to avoid these errors. We identify an additional class of distance-reducing hooks, which we call junction hook errors, that is not captured by this classification. The two data qubits of a junction hook error terminate anyon strings of two different colors at the point where the three colored strings of a logical operator meet, which we call the junction. As such, the hook error does not correspond to a single type of anyon, yet it still reduces the distance. For distances $d=7,9,11$, we verify that every non-corner plaquette admits a scheduled hook error that, together with $d-2$ single data qubit faults, produces an undetectable logical error using $d-1$ faults. Each of these is a junction hook error. Because of this, every stabilizer, including those in the bulk, still requires additional protection from hook errors (such as flag qubits) to restore the full circuit-level distance with this schedule. A minimum weight logical operator has at most one junction, however, so junction hook errors cannot accumulate on one minimum weight logical operator in the way that single-color malign hook errors can. This helps explain why the schedule still improves the circuit-level distance to $d-\lfloor (d+3)/6\rfloor$, rather than the $d/2$ distance associated with spatially uniform color code scheduling. △ Less

Unlike many existing metrics in the literature, our proposed metric incorporates the spatial locations of unmatched points. Drawing on both this new metric and existing metrics from the literature, we identify a set of common structural properties and develop a general framework for Wasserstein distances between point-process distributions based on metrics satisfying these properties.     Effective Dual Subgradient Methods Through Sequential Subgradient-Aligned Rays

    Authors: Hansi Abeynanda, Chathuranga Weeraddana, Carlo Fischione

    Abstract: …decision variable, a widely used problem formulation in many large-scale real-world applications. In particular, the SSAR method improves the convergence performance of the corresponding dual decomposition method while also reducing its communication cost. Numerical experiments on a least squares regression problem and a logistic regression problem using rea… ▽ More In this paper, we propose an efficient subgradient-based framework, referred to as the sequential subgradient-aligned rays (SSAR) method, for improving the convergence performance of the dual subgradient method. In particular, the proposed method identifies and exploits a finite sequence of rays, when available, as the dual subgradient method evolves, along each of which a subgradient of the negative dual function remains constant and aligns with the ray. While the dual variable iterates remain on each identified ray, a constant stepsize associated with the ray is employed, with the associated constant stepsizes decreasing from one ray to the next. This approach yields constant decrements in the negative dual function values while the dual variable iterates remain on the associated rays, enabling the dual variable iterates to progress more quickly toward a dual optimal solution. We then show that the proposed SSAR method can be leveraged to improve the performance of the dual decomposition method for minimizing the average of local loss functions associated with multiple agents that share a common decision variable, a widely used problem formulation in many large-scale real-world applications. In particular, the SSAR method improves the convergence performance of the corresponding dual decomposition method while also reducing its communication cost. Numerical experiments on a least squares regression problem and a logistic regression problem using real datasets are conducted to evaluate the performance of the SSAR method. △ Less

    Optimal Search Strategies under Finite Resource Constraints

    Authors: Hugues Meyer

    Abstract: …the agent has continuous access to its past and current positions, which we find to always require resource allocation to be spatially concentrated. We then determine the corresponding protocols for blind searchers and show that resource usage must be more evenly distributed. Non-Markovian cues that generate environmental memory are found to substantially im… ▽ More Improving target search is a challenge faced by many natural and artificial agents, often requiring the use of energetic or material resources. These inputs may be supplied by an external controller or generated by the searcher itself, for example, through the deposition of cues along its trajectory. Although such resources are commonly modeled as costly but available without an explicit limit, in practice, they can often only be produced in finite amounts. Here, we go beyond the assumption of an unlimited supply of resources by investigating searches in which a finite resource budget must be allocated throughout the search process. To investigate this problem, we equip a one-dimensional diffusive searcher with three types of resources: an external force, a supply of partially reflecting flags, and auto-chemotactic cues. We derive exact optimal resource-allocation protocols for informed search processes in which the agent has continuous access to its past and current positions, which we find to always require resource allocation to be spatially concentrated. We then determine the corresponding protocols for blind searchers and show that resource usage must be more evenly distributed. Non-Markovian cues that generate environmental memory are found to substantially improve search performance when the agent has access to positional information, even under finite-resource constraints. In contrast, their benefits are considerably more limited for blind search, particularly when the cues provide no directional information. △ Less

    eess.SY

    On the Discernibility of Topology Changes in Networked LTI Systems

    Authors: Giulio Fattore, Maria Elena Valcher

    Abstract: …undirected communication graphs and show that, under mild additional assumptions, discernibility modulo synchronization is equivalent to the dynamical discernibility of the corresponding topology change. This establishes a direct connection between the two notions and extends existing results on the discernibility of topology variations in dynamical networks… ▽ More This article investigates the discernibility of topology changes in networked linear time-invariant systems, with particular focus on edge disconnection. We introduce a new notion of discernibility motivated by a limitation of the definition commonly adopted in the literature. In consensus networks, topology changes are inherently indiscernible for all initial conditions belonging to the synchronization subspace. To overcome this issue, we first introduce the notion of dynamical discernibility of a topology change for elementary consensus networks. This notion explicitly disregards the intrinsic indiscernibility associated with states in the consensus subspace. We characterize this property for directed communication graphs and then specialize the resulting conditions to topology changes arising from either the disconnection of an edge or a variation in its weight. We then extend the analysis to networks of homogeneous agents by introducing discernibility modulo synchronization, which factors out the unavoidable indiscernibility associated with synchronized trajectories. We derive a general characterization showing how discernibility depends jointly on the topology change and on the dynamics of the individual agents. Finally, we specialize the analysis to undirected communication graphs and show that, under mild additional assumptions, discernibility modulo synchronization is equivalent to the dynamical discernibility of the corresponding topology change. This establishes a direct connection between the two notions and extends existing results on the discernibility of topology variations in dynamical networks. The resulting characterizations are then further specialized to the case of edge disconnection. △ Less
